#7158. Senate appropriations committee proposes $15B in additional Pell Grant funding

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Republican-led bill for fiscal 2027 would also keep funding flat for the U.S. Department of Education’s Office for Civil Rights.

Congress has until Dec. 11 to either pass a budget or another stopgap funding measure to avoid a government shutdown.

But the midterm election results have increased the pressure on Republicans, who currently control both chambers of Congress. If lawmakers pass another continuing resolution that extends into the new year or if the government shuts down until January, power over the budget will be handed to Democrats.

Against that backdrop, West Virginia Sen. Shelley Moore Capito, chair of the appropriations subcommittee overseeing Education Department funding, said Monday that the bill reflects “priorities that have long enjoyed bipartisan support.”

The Education Department would receive $93.9 billion in discretionary funding in fiscal 2027 under the proposal, a major boost from the $78.8 billion appropriated to the agency in the prior year.

The bill would keep funding for the department’s Office for Civil Rights at $140 million, while Trump’s fiscal 2027 proposal sought to cut OCR’s funding to $91 million. Similarly, the Institute of Education Sciences, the department’s office that collects and analyzes data from colleges and K-12 schools, would get $789.6 million, the same as it did in fiscal 2026. The White House proposed just $261.3 million.

But the education funding bill — released alongside budget proposals for several other federal agencies — left the committee’s Democratic leadership dissatisfied. In a Monday statement, Washington Sen. Patty Murray, vice chair of the appropriations committee, heavily criticized the overall budget bill as being full of “backward funding priorities,” citing the significant increases in defense spending.

She also argued the proposal “fails to rein in this administration” and prevent Trump from politicizing federal grants and moving to dismantle the Education Department.

Murray urged her party members to join her in negotiating with Republicans.

“Democrats cannot accept a topline agreement that delivers a windfall for defense contractors and crumbs for everyone else,” she said.

At least one higher ed group has also urged lawmakers to go further.

Nalia Medina, assistant director of government relations at the National Association of Student Financial Aid Administrators, said Wednesday that it was encouraging to see a proposal to address the Pell Grant program’s shortfall. But she also called for an increase to the maximum Pell Grant, citing its decreased spending power.

“Today’s students are still struggling,” she said in a statement. “As inflation continues to increase, housing and food expenses take up a larger portion of students’ budgets.”

#7154. AI Native Creative Strategist - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

What if your next playground was to build a global, iconic Brand, with a creative machine that never sleeps, and compounds?

We’re not looking for a "Social Media Manager" to follow a calendar. We’re looking for an AI-native Creative Strategist (Brand & Content).

Someone who understands that to win today, you don’t just need "content", you need a Movement.

This isn’t another content job. Most content teams use AI to write captions faster. That’s the 99%. We want to be in the 0.1%: a media where AI runs the creative loop, from the signal to the hook, the cut, the post and the lesson. Every day, on every platform, in French and in English. You bring the taste. The machine you build brings the scale.

Here’s the bet: a company that learns by itself grows in a way a company of people alone can’t. We aim for $10M in revenue with 10 people, each of them running a dozen agents.

Paradoxically, the more AI runs our content, the more human it has to feel. AI doesn’t make more of what everyone makes. It makes more of what only we can make.

1. Read the signals. Comments, DMs, retention graphs, saves, shares, search trends, objections from sales calls, and what the best creators and advertisers in the world are publishing right now.

2. Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, years of interviews, lives and courses, member stories, and every past post with what it taught us, in one shared company brain.

3. Generate in our voice. Hooks, scripts, shorts, carousels, cuts, thumbnails and captions, in French and in English.

4. Ship. Format, schedule, publish and answer, on every platform.

5. Measure with real KPIs. Retention, shares, saves, follower growth, and the followers who become leads and members. Not likes.

6. Learn and loop on its own. Double down on what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and start the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

This is already real. An ads library of thousands of creatives feeds our creative research. Agents cut ads from long-form content and wire Instagram comment-to-DM automations. And our product is AI-native too: Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day.

At Paradox, we are convinced that greatness is trainable, and we believe self-help is broken.

Most learning platforms are used the same way: consuming generic theories, getting quick highs, ending with no real change.

People deserve more: a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges, and grows with them.

That’s why we want to empower people at scale, by leveraging cutting edge technologies like AI, and crafting user experiences with outstanding aesthetics.

- Human Intelligence (coaches, peers, and mentors)

- Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

- Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

To create one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world.

You lead the Content Factory not by management, but with creativity.

In a classic content team, people do the work and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the volume, you design the machine, set the bar and make the calls. Humans do what no machine can: taste.

Build a symbiotic strategy where David Laroche’s personal brand and Paradox’s company brand reinforce each other without cannibalization.

Implement radical new formats (documentaries, conceptual shorts) that shatter the boring codes of the "Self-Help" industry.

Define distinct, sharp editorial lines for each platform: Depth & Search for YouTube, Aesthetic & Vibe for Instagram, and Authority & Authenticity for LinkedIn.

❌ The old way: a strategy deck written once a year, and a calendar filled by habit.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents watch every platform, every competitor and every comment continuously. They tell you which angle is rising, which format is tiring and which story our audience asks for. The strategy updates every week, on evidence.

Take responsibility for the entire production chain from hooks to storyboarding the narrative, and ensuring the final cut matches the vision.

Own the packaging: titles, thumbnails and the first 3 seconds. You test variants every week and know your click-through and retention numbers by heart.

❌ The old way: one shoot becomes one video and a handful of posts.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: one shoot, one live or one interview becomes dozens of hooks, shorts, carousels and ad cuts by the next morning, in both languages. Concepts are tested with AI-generated drafts before we spend a day shooting them.

Your taste becomes the eval. You write down what makes a great hook, a great cut, a great carousel and a great story, in rules and examples the agents check themselves against. Each time you reject something, the reason goes back into the rules.

Act as the final quality check: nothing gets published if it doesn’t meet the "Paradox Standard" of aesthetics and storytelling, whether a person or an agent made it.

❌ The old way: a creative team briefs an editor, waits three days and gets two versions.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents draft, cut, caption, translate and schedule. You keep the best, and next week their first drafts are already closer to your bar.

Analyze retention graphs, CTR, and CPL to iterate based on feedback, killing what fails and aggressively scaling what works.

❌ The old way: someone pulls the stats at the end of the month and nobody changes anything.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents read the numbers of every post every day, explain why it worked or didn’t, and propose the next test. You measure the machine itself: posts per week, time from idea to live, hit rate, and the share of the work done without a human.

We started in French. You build the machine that takes Paradox to English-speaking audiences: channels, formats and voice that feel native, not translated.

And always: real stories told with consent, no fake claims, no fake scarcity. The machine can scale, but it doesn’t decide what’s right. You do.

#7152. AI Native Creative Director - EdTech (French fluent 🇫🇷) at Paradox

What if your next playground was to build a global, iconic Brand, with a creative machine that never sleeps, and compounds?

We’re not looking for a "Social Media Manager" to follow a calendar. We’re looking for an AI-native Creative Director (Brand & Content).

Someone who understands that to win today, you don’t just need "content", you need a Movement.

This isn’t another content job. Most content teams use AI to write captions faster. That’s the 99%. We want to be in the 0.1%: a media where AI runs the creative loop, from the signal to the hook, the cut, the post and the lesson. Every day, on every platform, in French and in English. You bring the taste. The machine you build brings the scale.

Here’s the bet: a company that learns by itself grows in a way a company of people alone can’t. We aim for $10M in revenue with 10 people, each of them running a dozen agents.

Paradoxically, the more AI runs our content, the more human it has to feel. AI doesn’t make more of what everyone makes. It makes more of what only we can make.

1. Read the signals. Comments, DMs, retention graphs, saves, shares, search trends, objections from sales calls, and what the best creators and advertisers in the world are publishing right now.

2. Hold the full context of Paradox. Our DNA, years of interviews, lives and courses, member stories, and every past post with what it taught us, in one shared company brain.

3. Generate in our voice. Hooks, scripts, shorts, carousels, cuts, thumbnails and captions, in French and in English.

4. Ship. Format, schedule, publish and answer, on every platform.

5. Measure with real KPIs. Retention, shares, saves, follower growth, and the followers who become leads and members. Not likes.

6. Learn and loop on its own. Double down on what works, kill what doesn’t, write the lesson back into the brain, and start the next cycle without waiting for a meeting.

This is already real. An ads library of thousands of creatives feeds our creative research. Agents cut ads from long-form content and wire Instagram comment-to-DM automations. And our product is AI-native too: Athena, our AI coach, works with members every day.

At Paradox, we are convinced that greatness is trainable, and we believe self-help is broken.

Most learning platforms are used the same way: consuming generic theories, getting quick highs, ending with no real change.

People deserve more: a system that knows them, one that adapts, challenges, and grows with them.

That’s why we want to empower people at scale, by leveraging cutting edge technologies like AI, and crafting user experiences with outstanding aesthetics.

- Human Intelligence (coaches, peers, and mentors)

- Structured Intelligence (protocols, mental models, rituals)

- Technological Intelligence (AI personalization, feedback loops, adaptive journeys)

To create one seamless ecosystem: Paradox OS.

Paradox was founded by David Laroche, a creator followed by over 2 million people and known for his interviews with icons like Tony Parker, Mike Horn, Richard Branson and Gad Elmaleh. We started in French. We’re building for the world.

You lead the Content Factory not by management, but with creativity.

In a classic content team, people do the work and AI helps. Here it’s the other way round: agents do the volume, you design the machine, set the bar and make the calls. Humans do what no machine can: taste.

Build a symbiotic strategy where David Laroche’s personal brand and Paradox’s company brand reinforce each other without cannibalization.

Implement radical new formats (documentaries, conceptual shorts) that shatter the boring codes of the "Self-Help" industry.

Define distinct, sharp editorial lines for each platform: Depth & Search for YouTube, Aesthetic & Vibe for Instagram, and Authority & Authenticity for LinkedIn.

❌ The old way: a strategy deck written once a year, and a calendar filled by habit.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents watch every platform, every competitor and every comment continuously. They tell you which angle is rising, which format is tiring and which story our audience asks for. The strategy updates every week, on evidence.

Take responsibility for the entire production chain from hooks to storyboarding the narrative, and ensuring the final cut matches the vision.

Own the packaging: titles, thumbnails and the first 3 seconds. You test variants every week and know your click-through and retention numbers by heart.

❌ The old way: one shoot becomes one video and a handful of posts.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: one shoot, one live or one interview becomes dozens of hooks, shorts, carousels and ad cuts by the next morning, in both languages. Concepts are tested with AI-generated drafts before we spend a day shooting them.

Your taste becomes the eval. You write down what makes a great hook, a great cut, a great carousel and a great story, in rules and examples the agents check themselves against. Each time you reject something, the reason goes back into the rules.

Act as the final quality check: nothing gets published if it doesn’t meet the "Paradox Standard" of aesthetics and storytelling, whether a person or an agent made it.

❌ The old way: a creative team briefs an editor, waits three days and gets two versions.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents draft, cut, caption, translate and schedule. You keep the best, and next week their first drafts are already closer to your bar.

Analyze retention graphs, CTR, and CPL to iterate based on feedback, killing what fails and aggressively scaling what works.

❌ The old way: someone pulls the stats at the end of the month and nobody changes anything.

🏴‍☠️ At Paradox: agents read the numbers of every post every day, explain why it worked or didn’t, and propose the next test. You measure the machine itself: posts per week, time from idea to live, hit rate, and the share of the work done without a human.

We started in French. You build the machine that takes Paradox to English-speaking audiences: channels, formats and voice that feel native, not translated.

#7150. Senior Product Designer - Product Growth at Kit

Kit is the email marketing platform experts choose to grow their audience, deepen their relationships, and build a business around the work they love. For coaches, YouTubers, authors, podcasters, and other creatives, there isn't a better marketing hub to rely on to grow audiences, automate email marketing, and sell digital products — all within one platform.More importantly, there isn't a team more committed to helping creators earn a living. We're on a mission to help creators earn $1 billion using our creator marketing platform. We have always been 100% independent and 100% remote.We are proud to have built a product that our customers love, and we look for people who have enthusiasm and belief in our mission, vision, and values to join our team. We're also embracing AI thoughtfully — both in how we build and how we hire to ensure our team is adaptable, innovative, and ready for what's next.

We’re looking for a senior product designer to be the first dedicated designer on our product growth team, shaping how creators get set up, see value in their first days, and enjoy a seamless upgrade experience. Kit ships fast, and the product growth team builds the frameworks and systems that connect creators to the value in their plan as the product keeps changing. We need a designer who can balance rapid experimentation with long-term value for our creators.

Product growth is a new team at Kit, formed earlier this year. In the six months since, we’ve found our rhythm and started to move the numbers, but we’re still shaping how we work, how we prioritise and where we focus in the long term. As the team’s designer, you’ll be the design voice for growth, with real influence over how the team works and what it builds.

You'll own the design of the moments that move our funnel: onboarding, activation, free trials, upgrade and upsell flows, pricing and checkout, and billing. You'll work in a team that learns by shipping, through A/B tests and ship-and-measure changes, and you'll use data to decide what to try next.

This role looks beyond the team. You’ll keep a pulse on what other product teams are shipping and how marketing communicates, so that what creators are promised before sign up matches what they find during onboarding. We’re looking for someone who enjoys looking across Kit to see what's changing, then focusing on what it means for their own work.

This is a full-time IC role for a designer who loves connecting design decisions to numbers, moves quickly without sacrificing quality, and is excited about using AI to accelerate outcomes.

We need someone who thinks deeply about user behaviour, interaction design and the product experience, and who is proactive about finding the next opportunity rather than waiting for a spec.

You'll report to Dan Winer, Director of Product Design, and join a small, high-impact product design team currently consisting of four senior product designers and a senior design engineer. From day one, you’ll be part of the product growth team’s product trio alongside the product manager and engineering manager, shaping the team’s vision, priorities and how we work. You’ll also work closely with the team’s engineers.

You’ll work closely with product marketing on how we talk to creators before and after they sign up, and with the other product designers to stay ahead of what’s shipping across Kit. You’ll also partner with data and our creator success teams.

First week: Get to know Kit’s product and the product growth team’s goals. Get access to Figma, Linear, Mixpanel and our other reporting tools. Take part in get-to-know-you calls with the team.

First month: Get up to speed on how we track and measure creator behaviour in Mixpanel, Omni and Clarity, which experiments are running now, and what we’ve learnt from past ones. Join the product trio’s planning and prioritisation discussions, and ship a small experiment or two, with support from the Director of Product Design as you take ownership of the growth work.

First six months: Own design across the team’s work, which spans growth (onboarding, activation, trials and upgrades) and billing (checkout, plan changes, downgrades and cancellation). This might include personalising onboarding, improving free-to-paid conversion or simplifying our payment flows. By six months, the team should rely on you for a clear picture of how creators move through these flows, where they get stuck and why. You’ll help the team run more and better experiments, from forming a hypothesis to reading the results. We don’t have dedicated researchers, so you’ll talk to creators, run usability tests, track the impact of your work and proactively suggest iterations.

First year: Help the team improve our activation, free-to-paid, trial conversion, and early retention rates, so that more creators upgrade, stay on Kit, and find real value. Turn our upsell, discount and plan change moments into one coherent experience instead of a set of one-off prompts.

Interaction design for complex web applications, with a strong eye for visual hierarchy and detail.

Grounding design decisions in the creator’s voice through interviews, usability testing and customer feedback, partnering with the PM so it shapes what we build as well as how.

Designing flows that change depending on who’s seeing them, like free, trial and paid accounts, or creators upgrading and downgrading.

Designing onboarding, activation, upgrade, pricing, checkout or billing flows.

Experiment design and reading results, including knowing when an A/B test is the right approach or when to just ship and track.

Defining what events to track so the team can measure what you shipped, using tools like Mixpanel.

Clear written and visual communication of design rationale, including async walkthroughs.

Around 5+ years as a product designer on SaaS or other complex web-based products.

Led design on growth, monetisation or onboarding work and can point to its impact on metrics, ideally including retention or quality of conversion, not just volume.

Worked in a cross-functional team with a product manager and engineers, shipping continuously.

Worked within and contributed to an established design system.

A portfolio that shows how research or data informed your decisions and what happened as a result.

Bonus: designed pricing, billing or plan change experiences.

Curious about why users behave the way they do, and about what the numbers aren’t telling you.

Comfortable being wrong in public and sharing what you don’t know, not just what you do.

Proactive and self-directed, with a bias for shipping.

Excited about AI and pragmatic about how to use it.

Low ego and comfortable giving and receiving direct feedback.

Preferring to execute designs handed down from a product manager.

Treating a conversion win as the finish line, without checking whether those creators stay with Kit and find value.

Wanting long, highly-refined projects with little contact with data or results.

Discomfort with experiments that fail or with changing direction based on what we learn.

Focusing on visual polish without deeper thinking about user behaviour and business outcomes.

Being philosophically opposed to using AI in your work.

Needing well-established processes and a fully defined roadmap from day one.

We're 100% remote with team members across the globe. We work mostly on East Coast time - so if you're based on the West Coast of the United States, Europe, or Asia, you might have a few early mornings or late evenings. We do our best to keep things reasonable, but a little time zone juggling comes with the territory.

Kit has standardized salaries based on position, no matter where you live. For this role, we're hiring at level 4 ($172,000). Level is determined based on experience and our interview process.

We cover up to $2,280 per month toward medical premiums, with dental and vision premiums fully covered. We offer Health Insurance plans through Aetna

$2,000 equipment allowance for your first two years, $1,000 budget every following two years. Company-provided laptops are issued to every Kit team member and are not included in the equipment budget

Individual learning + development budget ($3,500/year)

Creator meetup budget ($50/month) to connect with creators over coffee, lunch, or other in-person meetups

Childcare benefit up to $3,000 annually through JOON

Twenty (20) days of paid time off during each year of employment

Paid paid vacation: An after-tax bonus of $1,000 for taking five consecutive days of vacation where you’re fully unplugged from work

Two weeks of paid sick time each year, including mental health + well being days

Twelve (12) weeks paid parental leave and flexible scheduling in your child’s first year

Up to six weeks of paid bereavement leave, medical leave, and disaster after six months of employment, two weeks of each paid leave in your first six months

Winter Break Closure: Kit closes for a week at the end of December, giving everyone a collective break to enjoy the holiday season. Essential support services remain available, with teams coordinating to ensure coverage during this period

Four-week, paid sabbatical after five years with the team

Fantastic in-person or virtual retreats with the team twice a year

#7147. Texas Democrats propose bills to nix state DEI ban at public colleges

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The party clinched the state’s House and governorship in the midterm elections, though that still may not be enough to get the legislation passed.

In the state’s House, Reps. Nicole Collier and John Bucy III have put forward bills that would repeal the 2023 law. Texas state Sen. José Menéndez introduced a companion bill in the state’s Senate.

At any other time in the past three decades, the new bills might have faced little chance of passage simply by virtue of being championed by Democrats. But the party now controls two-thirds of a trifecta for the first time since 1996 — a makeup that may still not be enough to pass the legislation.

Senate Bill 17 — the target of the new bills — bans several DEI activities at public colleges, including:

Texas was one of the first states to ban DEI offices and initiatives in higher education. Following SB 17 and a similar law in Florida, universities from Ohio to Iowa to Alaska shuttered their DEI offices, often under legislative mandates.

Institutions quickly responded to SB 17. For instance, the University of North Texas said before the law took effect that it would restructure its Multicultural Center and Pride Alliance into the student affairs office and launch a new Center for Belonging and Engagement.

After passage, the Texas A&M University System issued guidance stating that DEI terminology couldn’t be used on the institution’s websites. Offices dedicated to student affinity communities would have to close, though it said some programs and libraries could be absorbed into broader offices and initiatives.

Texas Tech University also closed its DEI office.

Texas Tech University System’s latest chancellor — Brandon Creighton,  who helped author SB 17 — has gone even further than the bill’s requirements, which don’t cover academic content. The system has moved to eliminate academic programs focused on sexual orientation and gender identity and put restrictions on teaching about race, sex and gender in courses.

State audits so far have determined that the University of Texas System, nearly all of the institutions within theTexas A&M University System and 19 of the state’s community colleges are compliant with SB 17.

Audits last year deemed two institutions noncompliant — a community college because it made a new employee participate in a DEI training and Texas A&M-Central Texas because it struck a contract that didn’t conform to the law’s ban.

#7146. Education Department faces management challenges from recent changes, watchdog says

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An annual federal report cited interagency agreements and a handful of executive orders as key factors that will challenge the agency in 2027.

Since early 2025, the second Trump administration has outsourced many of the Education Department’s core functions such as grant management, monitoring and technical assistance to other agencies through interagency agreements. The department has also reorganized many of its offices and laid off a bulk of its staff.

"Change of this magnitude can result in challenges associated with employee resistance, fatigue, and uncertainty — such as decreased productivity, operational disruption, and lost efficiency — and have an impact on the organization’s ability to respond to stakeholder questions and concerns," the Nov. 13 report from OIG stated.

The Education Department has not yet issued an annual performance plan for fiscal year 2026 and has yet to issue its annual performance report for FY 2025, strategic plan for FY 2026-2030, or annual performance plan for FY 2027, according to the report.

Other reports through various federal offices have flagged similar concerns with the Education Department's output amid McMahon's efforts to close the agency, which ultimately requires Congressional approval.

In June, another OIG report found that layoffs at the Education Department appear to have impacted units that were performing legal duties.

The staffing changes were made to 16 of the department's 17 offices, resulting in an overall reduction of at least 40% of the more than 4,000 employees who were working for the Education Department when Trump reentered office in January 2025.

Many lawmakers, civil rights groups and lawyers have raised concerns with output from at least one of those offices, the Office for Civil Rights, which saw a slowdown in case resolutions that included no sexual harassment or assault cases being settled in 2025. A later report from the office of Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., showed that OCR resolved only 1% of its cases overall in 2025, the lowest percentage from any OCR in over the last decade.

Responding to a draft of the most recent OIG report, the Education Department said it had made progress in many of the areas of concern, including change management. According to OIG, the department said it has "continued efforts to improve operational processes and maintain program delivery during a period of substantial organizational change." Additionally, the agency said it has "engaged in contractor support to assist with resolution for external audits," among other adjustments.

The department has previously maintained that its staffing and organizational changes will not affect the statutory functions it is legally obliged to perform.

The department also stressed in a comment to K-12 Dive that the OIG report is an annual update. In a January OIG report, the office flagged similar oversight challenges stemming from executive orders and resulting organizational changes.

"It is critical that the Department strategically plan for and effectively manage these and other major operational changes that it may decide to undertake," it warned at the time.

Past reports have flagged challenges under previous administrations, such as monitoring grantees' use of COVID-19 pandemic relief funding.

#7143. 29 states will require finance course in high school by 2031, report predicts

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Ten states received an “A” for requiring standalone personal finance courses in the National Endowment for Financial Education’s latest report.

“We’re excited about the progress this year’s report shows,” said Taylor Stanek, managing director of policy and advocacy for NEFE. “There is momentum and movement behind the effort to create a national expectation that students receive financial education in high school.”

The center's research involved reviews of each states' approach to personal finance education in high schools, state academic standards on the topic, and related laws, guidelines and regulations.

Broadly speaking, states with an “A” grade have shown they are implementing financial education with fidelity, while those receiving a “B” or “C” offer some level of financial education but short of a full semester course. These states also may have some degree of requirements in statewide academic standards.

States receiving a “D” or “F” have little or nothing to offer at the moment, Stanek said, expressing optimism that all 50 states will receive an “A” eventually.

“There are a few things we are keeping a close eye on as states move forward,” he said. “Implementation is key; passing one of these requirements is just a start."

The states also need to make sure schools are offering the courses as expected, as a standalone commitment, with teachers who have been trained, and have curricular materials available to meet the needs of specific students, Stanek said.

"This is an opportunity for every state … to make sure they’re evaluating the effectiveness of these programs and maximizing their benefit and impact,” Stanek said. Each state takes a somewhat different approach based on their unique range of needs, he added.

Utah implemented the first semester-long financial education requirement in 2008 and conducts statewide audits on programs, while Missouri was the second state in 2010, Stanek said. Even states with “C” grades or lower have seen progress in certain localities, with Denver Public Schools adopting this ahead of the state of Colorado, for example, he said.

“In the majority of other states, there’s at least something in place,” he added. “It’s ensuring that it’s offered across the board — requiring it to make sure every student receives it. When it’s an elective, students of color, low-income students and female students are less likely to take it.”

The figures from the 2025 national opinion poll underscore why the momentum has grown, especially at a time of economic uncertainty, Stanek said. “There’s a recognition that people need extra support to be [financially] successful,” he said.

“This is no longer a niche side topic. Folks are saying this is something worthy of being a core academic discipline in our K-12 system.”

The previous five editions of the report were published by the Center for Financial Literacy at Champlain College in Burlington, Vt.

#7134. How can districts keep CTE programs relevant as tech evolves?

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The rapid pace of change can make it difficult for even employers to have clear answers for workplace readiness, an industry expert says.

How can leaders of high school career and technical education programs ensure those offerings remain relevant in preparing students to adapt in a rapidly changing workforce?

“That question is one everyone is trying to answer right now. I’m not sure there is one clear answer,” said Alisha Hyslop, chief policy, research and content officer at the Association for Career and Technical Education.

Hyslop said the main piece of advice for educators is to work closely with businesses and industries to ensure programs are preparing students with the skills they’ll need to be successful — and that’s probably the same, at least on the surface, as it would have been 10 or 20 years ago.

But there are important distinctions in 2026, Hyslop said.

Because technology is changing so rapidly, employers don’t always have a clear answer for workplace readiness approaches, she said.

Hyslop said ACTE is also hearing that students need to understand basic, foundational artificial intelligence skills and how to use them. Additionally, students should continue to "build up durable skills," which is "really critical," she said.

Despite employers’ own lack of clarity at times, regular conversations should definitely continue, Hyslop said.

“There should be a give-and-take between the CTE educators, and folks working on curriculum and instruction in CTE, and the employer community,” she said. And although technology will continue to rapidly evolve, “students are still going to need critical thinking and communications skills, and the ability to work in teams, and evaluate information — and all of those foundational skills that apply across technologies.”

CTE programs focused on information technology are changing the most rapidly, although Hyslop said she has seen plenty of research to show that across the spectrum of career fields, new AI tools are being implemented and deployed. One example is the use of the technology in the agriculture field to monitor soil health and predict crop growth, she said.

“We had some examples from a school in Bentonville [Ark.] that was using AI in health science programs. They had created a meal planning app for mothers with gestational diabetes, or for pregnant women who could get answers to specific questions; those were joint projects between healthcare and technology students," Hyslop said. "There were lots of applications in digital media, and lots of ethical considerations that students are exploring as they go through that process.”

While some industries are more impacted by the advent of AI and other changes in the workplace than others, at first glance, people might think it’s mainly an issue in technology classes, Hyslop said. “And that is not the case. Because we’re seeing applications across career areas, once students get into the workplace.”

During a panel discussion at a recent ACTE conference focused on the use of AI in CTE programs, Hyslop said, students emphasized how important it was that they learned foundational skills first.

One student pointed out the approach of using a calculator to solve math problems, she recalled. “But that’s not how we teach students in kindergarten. We teach them basic mathematical functions first, and then introduce the calculator to do more complicated things.”

By learning the foundational level skills in CTE programs, they could evaluate the answers given by AI more competently, the students said. For instance, a student studying automotive mechanics could examine AI-generated instructions on how to fix cars.

“If you had no real understanding of those instructions, you wouldn’t know if it made a mistake,” she said. “That was a really important takeaway from that panel — that even students realized that AI, or whatever the next technology is, is still going to need people to instruct, evaluate and modify all of these technologies.”

#7133. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

As generative AI has become embedded in the tools students use to search, write, create, translate, organize, and receive feedback, the boundary between "using AI" and "not using AI" has become increasingly difficult to define. More importantly, treating AI use as a binary choice does little to help students understand the different roles AI can appropriately play in learning.

Artificial intelligence is raising the stakes of cyber conflict as attackers use the technology to accelerate reconnaissance, uncover vulnerabilities, and launch attacks faster than many security teams can respond, according to a new report from agentic AI security company Kai.

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

#7130. Fewer than half of students are kindergarten-ready, GAO says

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A five-state analysis shows girls, as well as Asian and White children, were more prepared. Boys, Black students and those with disabilities were less so.

Positive factors associated with kindergarten readiness include parenting, home environment and students' learning-related skills and social behaviors. Negative factors may include health, socioeconomic status, gender, and race or ethnicity, the Nov. 12 report said.

GAO's analysis of seven of the 11 studies found that public preschool programs had different impacts on varying student demographic groups when compared to students who didn't attend such programs. For instance, in one Georgia district, a publicly funded preschool program led to "persistently positive effects" for children from lower-income families.

The five states studied — Connecticut, Florida, Kentucky, Maryland and Ohio — also showed variation in kindergarten readiness rates. That may be due in part to how the states measure readiness, administer their kindergarten entry assessments, or report the assessment results, GAO said.

Among the five states, kindergarten readiness rates also differed by district type. Suburban districts were generally closer to their state's average for kindergarten readiness, while rural and urban districts varied more.

Also at the local level, the analysis found better kindergarten readiness rates among school districts in Connecticut, Kentucky and Ohio that had higher rates of students enrolled in public or private preschool. But there was no statistically significant association in that regard in Florida or Maryland.

This month's GAO report follows a 2024 report from the government watchdog agency on how states and teachers use kindergarten readiness data.

The 2024-25 school year saw record highs in enrollment and funding for state-supported preschool nationwide, according to The State of Preschool 2025, a report released earlier this year by the National Institute for Early Education Research. However, the rate of enrollment growth slowed compared to the 2023-24 school year, and many states still were not back to pre-pandemic preschool enrollment rates, the NIEER report said.

#7128. AI Ambitions Are Running Into a Network Infrastructure Trust Problem

Enterprise ambitions around AI are running into a fundamental problem: Orgnanizations aren't always confident in the networks those projects depend on, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

The research found that concerns about network providers have caused organizations to rethink strategic initiatives over the past two years. Among respondents whose initiatives were affected, AI and data-driven projects were the most cited, at 49%. That put AI ahead of security and compliance programs at 44%, digital transformation at 42%, and cloud migration at 38%.

The findings come from Arelion's report, "The Trust Illusion: Why AI and Digital Transformation Need More Than a Reliable Network," based on a survey of 518 senior enterprise network decision-makers in the U.S., U.K., Germany, and France conducted by Savanta.

All respondents worked for organizations with more than 2,000 employees and influenced network strategy across data centers, cloud, and connectivity. Of those surveyed, 66% had final sign-off responsibility. The results suggest that as organizations pursue increasingly data-intensive AI projects, confidence in the network infrastructure supporting them could become another deployment obstacle.

On the surface, Arelion found little evidence of a trust crisis. A total of 93% of respondents said they largely or completely trust their current network provider, while 83% said that trust has strengthened over time.

The picture changes when respondents are asked about a serious network incident. Only 15% said they have complete confidence in their provider's ability to quickly detect and address such a problem. Another 42% said their provider seriously lets them down anywhere from a few times a year to monthly or more frequently.

Those concerns have consequences beyond the network itself. Responding organizations have delayed, scaled back, or stopped strategic initiatives because of provider trust concerns, while others have added resiliency or risk-mitigation measures.

AI and data projects were the most affected among respondents reporting an impact, as organizations try to move AI from experimentation into larger production deployments.

A serious provider failure could also have substantial consequences. Forty percent of respondents said such an event would cause major operational disruption, while 18% anticipated significant financial loss or severe financial or reputational damage.

Another tension runs through Arelion's findings. Enterprises need networks they can trust to support AI projects, but many of the same decision-makers are concerned about AI's growing role inside those networks. A total of 54% of respondents identified AI and automation in network management and security as the factors most likely to negatively affect network trust over the next three years. That ranked ahead of data sovereignty expectations and geopolitical instability, both at 39%, and regulatory requirements and quantum computing threats to current encryption standards, both at 38%.

At the same time, respondents aren't rejecting AI as a network technology. Proactive AI-driven fault prediction was among the capabilities they said would matter most to future trust, alongside stronger built-in security, greater accountability, and responsive support. That leaves enterprises in an unusual position: AI is increasing their dependence on digital infrastructure while also raising questions about how that infrastructure itself is managed.

Arelion has an interest in emphasizing the importance of network-provider trust, and the survey reflects the views specifically of network decision-makers at large enterprises. Still, the findings point to an infrastructure consideration that can get lost amid the rush to adopt new AI models and agents. AI projects ultimately depend on the networks, cloud environments, data centers, and connectivity beneath them. If organizations lack confidence in that foundation, access to better AI models alone won't remove the obstacle.

"Trust is influencing which strategic initiatives move forward, how organizations manage risk, and which providers they choose to work with," said Mattias Fridström, Vice President at Arelion. "As businesses become increasingly reliant on digital infrastructure, enterprises are looking for network providers that can demonstrate transparency, accountability, and operational excellence, not just availability."

The full report is available for download here at the Arelion site (registration required)

#7126. Here are the states with ESEA waivers from the Trump administration

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

We’re tracking the Returning Education to the States waivers granted by the U.S. Department of Education since January 2026.

Ten states have so far been granted waivers from certain federal education accountability, spending and other standards since January 2026. These moves come as the Trump administration encourages states to seek such waivers as it moves to downsize the federal footprint in education.

In announcing the first waiver — granted to Iowa in January —  the U.S. Education Department said it was "empowering state education officials to have more discretion over their federal education dollars." The Returning Education to the States waivers are being granted under the Elementary and Secondary Education Act.

“Under President Trump’s leadership, we will continue to identify avenues to reduce burdensome requirements and maximize flexibility for state leaders to invest in their students," U.S. Education Secretary Linda McMahon said in a statement.

However, critics say that in loosening accountability and oversight standards for states, the waivers risk equity for underserved students and opening the door for money to flow to school choice options rather than traditional public schools.

While the waivers and how states plan to use them vary, they consolidate or redirect millions of dollars in federal funding streams, and some reconfigure states' accountability performance factors.

The waivers are built upon the clear intent of the Education Department, under the direction of President Donald Trump, to close down the 46-year-old agency to the greatest extent legally possible.

In March 2025, on the heels of mass layoffs that gutted the agency's staff, Trump issued an executive order telling McMahon to “take all necessary steps to facilitate the closure of the Department of Education."

Since then, the department — steeped in litigation over those attempts —  has continuously stressed its goal to grant state waivers from what it calls "burdensome statutory and regulatory provisions."

Below, K-12 Dive is tracking the states that have received waivers and what flexibilities they allow.

#7122. Research: Lack of Specialized Infrastructure Is a Top AI Adoption Challenge

Enterprise AI projects are running into infrastructure questions as organizations move from planning toward deployment, according to recent research Digital Realty. Forty percent of survey respondents cited a lack of specialized infrastructure required by AI as a main challenge to adopting a formal AI strategy, up from 9% in the company's 2024 research.

The Global Data Insights Survey 2026 points to a widening mix of infrastructure models behind enterprise AI. Half of respondents host AI models in private clouds, 86% are pursuing or exploring sovereign AI, 92% tie data-location strategy to their AI plans, and 88% follow a distributed-data approach.

The research is based on an online survey of 2,131 IT decision-makers across 19 countries, and 11 industries, conducted from Nov. 23, 2025, through Feb. 4, 2026.

Organizations also appear to be shifting from AI planning toward execution. The share actively executing AI to achieve operational efficiency or reduce costs rose from 27% in 2024 to 37% this year. Only 3% reported no measurable ROI to date, while 63% expect returns within the next six months to two years.

Specialized infrastructure led the survey's list of AI adoption challenges at 40%, followed by regulatory and personal-data concerns at 35%. Data availability and quality, storage capacity, interconnection, compute resources, and staffing also ranked as significant constraints.

Those requirements are also shaping workload placement. Integration with existing IT or data infrastructure ranked first at 43%, while regulatory compliance and performance were each cited by 37%. Data proximity, sovereignty, sustainability, cost, and intellectual property protection also factored into hosting decisions.

Private cloud features prominently across the report's AI hosting categories, but enterprises are clearly using a mix of private cloud, public cloud, on-premises infrastructure, and colocation. Private cloud accounted for 50% of custom-built AI model hosting and 47% for open source or fine-tuned large language models (LLMs).

Sovereignty is another part of that placement decision. Thirty-two percent consider sovereign AI — in which data and AI models remain within a single national or regulatory jurisdiction — a requirement, while another 54% are exploring it. The findings point toward hybrid environments in which infrastructure location, governance and data control increasingly overlap.

#7118. Education Department faces management challenges from recent changes, watchdog says

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Interagency agreements and a handful of executive orders were cited in an annual report as key factors in five issues facing the agency in 2027.

Since early 2025, the second Trump administration has outsourced many of the Education Department’s core functions such as grant management, monitoring and technical assistance to other agencies through interagency agreements. The department has also reorganized many of its offices and laid off a bulk of its staff.

"Change of this magnitude can result in challenges associated with employee resistance, fatigue, and uncertainty — such as decreased productivity, operational disruption, and lost efficiency — and have an impact on the organization’s ability to respond to stakeholder questions and concerns," the Nov. 13 report from OIG stated.

The Education Department has not yet issued an annual performance plan for fiscal year 2026 and has yet to issue its annual performance report for FY 2025, strategic plan for FY 2026-2030, or annual performance plan for FY 2027, according to the report.

Other reports through various federal offices have flagged similar concerns with the Education Department's output amid McMahon's efforts to close the agency, which ultimately requires Congressional approval.

In June, another OIG report found that layoffs at the Education Department appear to have impacted units that were performing legal duties.

The staffing changes were made to 16 of the department's 17 offices, resulting in an overall reduction of at least 40% of the more than 4,000 employees who were working for the Education Department when Trump reentered office in January 2025.

Many lawmakers, civil rights groups and lawyers have raised concerns with output from at least one of those offices, the Office for Civil Rights, which saw a slowdown in case resolutions that included no sexual harassment or assault cases being settled in 2025. A later report from the office of Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., showed that OCR resolved only 1% of its cases overall in 2025, the lowest percentage from any OCR in over the last decade.

Responding to a draft of the most recent OIG report, the Education Department said it had made progress in many of the areas of concern, including change management. According to OIG, the department said it has "continued efforts to improve operational processes and maintain program delivery during a period of substantial organizational change." Additionally, the agency said it has "engaged in contractor support to assist with resolution for external audits," among other adjustments.

The department has previously maintained that its staffing and organizational changes will not affect the statutory functions it is legally obliged to perform.

The department also stressed in a comment to K-12 Dive that the OIG report is an annual update. In a January OIG report, the office flagged similar oversight challenges stemming from executive orders and resulting organizational changes.

"It is critical that the Department strategically plan for and effectively manage these and other major operational changes that it may decide to undertake," it warned at the time.

Past reports have flagged challenges under previous administrations, such as monitoring grantees' use of COVID-19 pandemic relief funding.

#7117. OpenAI Intros Dots, AI Agents that Continue Working Between User Conversations

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

OpenAI is also previewing specialist Dots for organizations, with separate identities and credentials for defined responsibilities. Those enterprise deployments are beginning with focused pilots. The company is working with Microsoft on integration with Agent 365's governance and security controls.

The broader ambition seems to be to make delegation last beyond a chat session. That change could reduce the need to repeatedly explain a project. It also makes supervision a continuing obligation: An instruction that was appropriate yesterday may no longer fit today's circumstances.

Users currently cannot inspect or directly edit individual Dot memories. Deleting a Dot deletes its own context, while files and conversations it created remain separately stored. Plugin permissions are shared across Dots, ChatGPT, ChatGPT Work, and Codex.

For an organization, that creates two related governance questions: what an agent can retrieve now, and what it already knows. Revoking a connection addresses the first without necessarily resolving the second.

OpenAI's safety account distinguishes authorized tasks that continue in the background from unsolicited "proactive research." The latter uses tools restricted to reading permitted sources. Those research tasks cannot directly send messages, change connected application content, or control a browser or desktop. Any subsequent action must pass the usual rules and checks.

A separate system, Auto-review, checks certain planned actions against instructions and safety requirements. OpenAI says the agent cannot modify the controls enforcing those checks.

"Dots can still make mistakes," the company cautions.

OpenAI's Dots system-card appendix acknowledges that persistence and multi-agent systems are not new. Its evaluations examine how continuing work affects authorization boundaries.

In one test involving successive related tasks, moderate scope violations increased from 8.6% to 19.7% when the number of intervening tasks doubled from five to 10. Examples included carrying information between unrelated tasks or editing a shared document beyond the intended scope. The company reported no severe breaches or exfiltration in that evaluation.

These are controlled test results, not measured failure rates in customer deployments. They nevertheless identify a problem that a successful demonstration cannot settle: whether an agent keeps the right boundaries as its workload accumulates.

According to OpenAI's DevDay recap, Dots are available to Pro and Business Premium users in eligible markets. Enterprise, Edu, and Healthcare access is an administrator-enabled beta, off by default.

The operational question is how organizations will keep delegated authority current. A continuing assignment needs a human owner who can revise its scope and decide when to stop it. Otherwise, the convenience of leaving work with an agent can outlast the reason it was authorized.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

#7114. Senate committee’s FY27 plan gives Education Department 19% boost

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Funding would increase for special education and stay level for Title I, according to a Senate Appropriations Committee proposal.

Key K-12 programs would be level-funded or slightly boosted for fiscal year 2027 under a budget plan proposed by the Republican-led Senate Appropriations Committee as the deadline looms for enacting a funding bill.

Appropriations for programs such as Title I, Part A for low-income school communities ($18.4 billion), Title III for English learners ($890 million) and safe schools and citizenship education ($431 million) would stay the same as FY26 appropriations. Funding for the Office for Civil Rights would remain at $140 million, according to the spending plan.

The proposal recommends increases for several other programs, such as an additional $50 million for special education education programs ($15.5 billion), $60 million more for charter school funding ($500 million), and $5 million more for 21st Century Community Learning Centers, which help fund after-school and summer activities ($1.3 billion).

Overall, the Senate Appropriations committee’s budget proposal recommends $93.9 billion for the Education Department in FY27, a 19% increase over FY26 appropriations.

The FY27 proposal "makes meaningful investments" in programs such as childcare and education, said Sen. Shelley Moore Capito, R-W.Va., in a Monday statement. Capito is chair of the Senate Appropriations Labor, Health and Human Services, Education and Related Agencies Subcommittee.

But the full committee's vice chair, Sen. Patty Murray, D-Wash., said in a Monday statement that the larger Labor, Health and Human Services, Education and Related Agencies proposed funding bill "fails to rein in this administration — to stop the dismantlement of the Department of Education, prevent [President Donald] Trump from politicizing every federal grant, and so much more. We need to invest way more to help students thrive, protect people’s health, and help families afford the basics, like child care and their heating bill.”

A separate budget proposal for the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services recommends raising funding for Head Start early childhood education programs by $50 million for a total of $12.4 billion.

FY27 began Oct. 1, but before the deadline, Congress enacted a continuing resolution to delay decisions for an overall final federal budget until Dec. 11. By that date, Congress will either need to finalize the FY27 appropriations or enact another continuing resolution. If lawmakers do neither, there could be a government shutdown.

In June, the House Appropriations Committee advanced its FY27 spending plan that recommended an overall allocation of $71 billion, or a 10% cut, to the Education Department. That proposal included a $2 billion cut for Title I funding, a $46 million boost for special education programs and the elimination of the Office of English Language Acquisition.

Both the House and Senate committees' bills differ from Trump's proposed budget, which sought to cut Education Department spending by 3% to $76.5 billion by consolidating or eliminating several programs, such as English learner supports and Teacher Quality Partnership grants.

The Trump administration's spending proposal also recommended the elimination and consolidation of several Individuals with Disabilities Education Act national programs like teacher training and technical assistance, but that recommendation is not reflected in the House or Senate committees' budget plans.

The paths for the House and Senate committees' spending bills are unclear given the advancing fiscal deadline and partisan politics at play, said Tara Thomas, senior government affairs manager at AASA, The School Superintendents Association, in a Nov. 17 post on AASA’s website.

Starting the first week of January, Democrats will be the majority party in both the House and Senate and will take over key committee leadership roles.

#7113. Storage Platform Engineer at Supabase

Supabase runs millions of Postgres databases, and storage performance is now one of the biggest levers on how fast, reliable, and affordable they are. We're building a software-defined, NVMe-over-Fabrics storage platform underneath Supabase Postgres. Postgres stays unmodified, and the new layer delivers near-local-NVMe performance with the durability and flexibility of networked storage.

As the senior engineer driving storage, you'll own this effort end to end: the architecture and build-vs-buy decisions, integration into our compute platform, and operating the system in production.

Your first goal is raw Postgres I/O performance at scale. In the longer term, the layer you build will provide the block-level snapshots and copy-on-write clones behind database branching, fast restores, and instant resizes. Because it's software-defined, it will also travel with us as we expand beyond a single cloud provider into multi-cloud and colocated infrastructure.

This is a foundational, high-ownership role. You'll set the technical direction for storage at Supabase and help grow the team around it.

Lead the architecture of Supabase's NVMe-oF storage platform, including evaluating and deciding between adopting existing software-defined storage and building on SPDK

Integrate networked block storage into our Kubernetes-based compute platform through CSI drivers and custom controllers, covering provisioning, attach/detach, resizing, and migration

Make Postgres fast on networked storage by profiling and tuning the full I/O path, from fsync, WAL, and checkpoints through the kernel block layer, NVMe/TCP, and the network

Design for durability and availability, including replication or erasure coding, failure domains, failover, and data-integrity verification

Build I/O isolation and QoS so that noisy neighbors can't degrade other tenants

Set performance and reliability SLOs, and build the benchmarking and observability to hold the system to them

Design the layer for block-level snapshots and copy-on-write clones, laying the groundwork for database branching

Keep the platform portable across AWS, other clouds, and bare-metal/colo

Write design docs, mentor engineers, and work closely with the Compute, Branching, Postgres, and Multicloud teams

Participate in on-call rotations, incident response, and post-incident reviews

Resolve escalated support issues, while also identifying and resolving systemic issues across the platform

Have 7+ years of software engineering experience, including significant time building and operating storage systems in production

Have built or operated distributed or software-defined block storage (for example Ceph RBD, Lightbits, Simplyblock, Portworx, LINSTOR/DRBD, or an in-house system)

Have hands-on experience with NVMe/TCP or NVMe-oF, including targets, initiators, multipath, and failover behavior

Have a deep understanding of Linux storage internals: the block layer, io_uring, page cache, O_DIRECT and fsync semantics, filesystems, and device-mapper

Write strong systems code in Go, plus C, C++, or Rust

Measure performance rigorously with fio, pgbench, eBPF, and perf, and can explain latency down to its individual components

Understand durability tradeoffs between replication, erasure coding, and consistency under failure

Have led technical direction on a complex system: design docs, tradeoffs, and bringing others along

Have experience with Kubernetes controllers and/or CSI drivers

Have been on call for large production systems

Communicate clearly in writing and thrive in async, globally distributed teams

Experience implementing block-level snapshots, thin provisioning, or copy-on-write cloning

Postgres internals knowledge (WAL, buffer manager, checkpointer), or familiarity with OrioleDB

Experience with AWS networking and instance storage at scale (ENA, placement, bandwidth limits)

Experience running storage on bare metal or in colo

Contributions to open source storage projects or the Linux kernel storage stack

#7112. Survey Identifies Data Governance Enforcement Gap for Agentic AI Tools

According to a new survey from identity security company Delinea, 99.7% of IT and security leaders said their organization has a formal policy governing what data AI tools and agents are permitted to access. Yet only about 51% said AI access is checked against those policies in real time. Perhaps more worrying is that only 19% said their organization could detect in real time when an agent last accessed data outside its intended scope.

That disconnect is at the center of Delinea's fall 2026 Identity Security Report, "The AI Enforcement Gap," which suggests companies have made quick progress establishing AI governance but have not necessarily built the technical controls needed to enforce it.

The findings come from a pair of surveys covering 2,254 IT and security leaders and 2,250 non-IT employees at organizations with at least 500 employees across the U.S, U.K, Germany, Australia, Singapore, UAE, France, and India.

All the organizations were using or piloting AI, while the employees surveyed used at least one AI tool for work.

The problem becomes more consequential as companies move from AI tools that primarily respond to users toward agents capable of taking actions on their behalf.

Eighty-seven percent of IT and security respondents said their organization had experienced or suspected of an incident during the past year in which an AI tool or agent accessed sensitive information beyond what was required for its task.

Delinea's research also suggests companies are giving agents access that can persist longer than necessary. Only 58% of IT and security leaders said they have mechanisms that automatically revoke or expire AI access when a session ends, and those controls do not necessarily cover every agent or tool. Forty-two percent said many agent credentials remain active until an audit.

Agents also are not always receiving their own narrowly defined permissions. Half of IT leaders said their organizations use the employee's existing permissions as a boundary for what an agent can access. That can effectively hand an agent years of accumulated access belonging to the person who launched it.

That is particularly significant because an AI agent does not necessarily behave like a traditional service account. A service account typically performs a predefined process, while an agent can choose different tools, actions and sequences at runtime depending on the situation.

"Service accounts run fixed scripts," said Mike Albrecht, associate director in the Risk Advisory Practice at Cross Country Consulting. "An AI agent decides what actions it's going to take at runtime."

The enforcement problem is not limited to autonomous agents.

The researchers found that 76% of employees surveyed had bypassed formal approval at some point to use AI tools with company data, applications or systems. And 48% said they always or extensively use AI tools without going through formal approval.

Meanwhile, 60% said they had felt pressure to use AI with sensitive or confidential information even when they were not sure doing so was permitted.

Executives were particularly likely to circumvent the process. Eighty-one percent of C-level respondents said they always or regularly bypass AI access approval, compared with 33% of intermediate-level employees.

The result is a widening gap between written AI governance and what companies can see and control. Delinea found that only 41% of IT leaders said all AI tools and agents accessing company data are actively monitored. When an AI tool or agent went outside its intended scope, 55% said it took at least a day to detect the incident, while 30% said detection took four days or longer.

That suggests the next phase of enterprise AI governance may depend less on writing additional rules and more on whether organizations can enforce existing ones while an agent is working.

"Most organizations cannot confidently answer three questions: What agents are running in our environment? What can they access? What have they actually done?" said Delinea CEO Art Gilliland. "An agent can begin a session with legitimate access and drift into something it was never meant to do."

The full report is available for download here.

Technology directors touch nearly everything that happens in a school system. Here are three strategies these IT leaders can use to navigate their many duties without losing strategic focus, influence, or sustainability.

Cloud Security Alliance's 2026 Top Threats report ranks identity as the leading cloud security concern, followed by two new AI-related risks.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

#7109. Software Engineer - Postgres Tooling at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, Postgres Tooling to join our CLI team and help build the open-source tools developers use to change their Postgres schemas safely. You’ll join as a co-maintainer of pg-delta, our schema diff and migration engine, and grow into leading it.

Given two databases, or a database and a folder of SQL files describing the schema you want, pg-delta works out the differences and generates the migration to get from one to the other. It powers supabase db diff, db pull, and declarative schemas, and it runs underneath Supabase’s platform, branching, and AI agent workflows. Every migration it generates is verified on a throwaway clone before it’s trusted, and it’s tested against 331 scenarios on PostgreSQL 14–18. We’re now working toward a stable, standalone 1.0 release for any Postgres developer, on Supabase or not.

This is a correctness-first role. The SQL this tool generates runs against production databases, so privilege safety and avoiding data loss are non-negotiable. It’s a great fit for someone who loves Postgres at the catalog level, enjoys compiler-style problems, and thrives in async, fast-paced environments.

Co-maintain pg-delta: review and merge changes, including PRs written by AI agents, and grow into its lead maintainer.

Own correctness: triage bug reports from GitHub and support, fix diff and dependency-ordering bugs, and extend test coverage across Postgres versions.

Make sure generated migrations are privilege-safe and never silently lose data, by detecting destructive changes and rewriting them into safe equivalents where possible.

Help take pg-delta to a stable 1.0: a public API with a semver contract, documentation, and broader coverage of Postgres extensions.

Work with the open-source community: triage issues, review outside contributions, and say no gracefully when a request doesn’t fit.

Work with the teams across Supabase that build on pg-delta, so migrations are generated and run the same way everywhere.

Write the design docs and technical strategy that set the tool’s direction, working async across teams.

Share the CLI team’s on-call rotation. Weekdays are covered follow-the-sun, and because we ship packaged software that users can roll back, pages are rare.

Have 8+ years of experience as a software engineer, including deep, hands-on work with PostgreSQL.

Know Postgres internals at the catalog level: pg_catalog, pg_depend, object ownership, grants and default privileges, RLS policies, and extension membership.

Understand DDL and migration semantics: which changes take locks, which can’t run inside a transaction, how dependencies determine ordering, and how to spot a destructive change.

Treat a migration that silently loosens access as a top-severity bug.

Write strong TypeScript, and design tests for correctness, such as differential, property-based, or corpus testing across multiple Postgres versions.

Are comfortable with compiler-style problems: dependency graphs, topological ordering, cycle handling, and diffing.

Can confidently review complex changes in an intricate codebase, including AI-generated PRs.

Have owned software in production with users in the loop: staged rollouts, error monitoring, and triaging real bug reports.

Have led a technical project across several teams, from an ambiguous start to production.

Communicate clearly in writing and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Nice to have, roughly in order of how much they’d help:

Have built or maintained schema diff or migration tooling (such as migra, pg-schema-diff, Atlas, pgroll, sqitch, Prisma Migrate, or Flyway/Liquibase), or worked with pg_dump internals.

Open-source maintainer experience: API contracts, semver, documentation, and community triage.

Familiarity with Postgres extensions such as pg_cron, pgmq, pg_partman, vault, pg_net, pgvector, or PostGIS.

Contributions to Postgres itself, or authorship of a Postgres extension.

Experience with Supabase or another managed Postgres platform.
